using Xunit;
using Calc;

namespace Calc.Tests
{
    public class CalculatorTest
    {
        [Fact]
        public void TestAddTwoPositiveIntegersReturnsCorrectSum()
        {
            // If two positive integers are provided (e.g., 2 and 3), then the method should return their mathematical sum (5). This verifies the basic 'adds two integers' functionality for standard positive inputs.
            var result = Calculator.Add(2, 3);
            Assert.Equal(5, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddTwoNegativeIntegersReturnsCorrectSum()
        {
            // If two negative integers are provided (e.g., -2 and -3), then the method should return their mathematical sum (-5). This ensures the logic correctly handles negative signs during addition.
            var result = Calculator.Add(-2, -3);
            Assert.Equal(-5, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddMixedSignIntegersReturnsCorrectSum()
        {
            // If one positive integer and one negative integer are provided (e.g., 10 and -5), then the method should return their mathematical sum (5). This tests the behavior when operands have different signs, effectively verifying subtraction logic within addition.
            var result = Calculator.Add(10, -5);
            Assert.Equal(5, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddZeroAndZeroReturnsZero()
        {
            // If both input integers are zero (0 and 0), then the method should return 0. This verifies the identity element for addition with zero.
            var result = Calculator.Add(0, 0);
            Assert.Equal(0, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddIntegerAndZeroReturnsOriginalInteger()
        {
            // If one integer is non-zero (e.g., 5) and the other is 0, then the method should return the non-zero integer (5). This should hold true regardless of which parameter (a or b) is zero.
            var result1 = Calculator.Add(5, 0);
            Assert.Equal(5, result1);
            
            var result2 = Calculator.Add(0, 5);
            Assert.Equal(5, result2);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddCommutativePropertyHolds()
        {
            // If two distinct integers are provided (e.g., 10 and 20), then the result of Add(10, 20) must equal the result of Add(20, 10). This verifies the commutative property of addition, ensuring order of operands does not affect the result.
            var result1 = Calculator.Add(10, 20);
            var result2 = Calculator.Add(20, 10);
            Assert.Equal(result1, result2);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddMaxValueAndNegativeOneReturnsCorrectSum()
        {
            // If the first integer is int.MaxValue and the second is -1, then the method should return 2,147,483,646 (int.MaxValue - 1). This verifies correct behavior at the upper boundary of the integer range without causing overflow.
            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MaxValue, -1);
            Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue - 1, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddMinValueAndPositiveOneReturnsCorrectSum()
        {
            // If the first integer is int.MinValue and the second is 1, then the method should return -2,147,483,647 (int.MinValue + 1). This verifies correct behavior at the lower boundary of the integer range without causing overflow.
            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MinValue, 1);
            Assert.Equal(int.MinValue + 1, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddLargePositiveIntegersReturnsCorrectSum()
        {
            // If two large positive integers are provided that do not exceed int.MaxValue when summed (e.g., 1,000,000 and 2,000,000), then the method should return 3,000,000. This ensures precision is maintained for larger magnitudes.
            var result = Calculator.Add(1_000_000, 2_000_000);
            Assert.Equal(3_000_000, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddLargeNegativeIntegersReturnsCorrectSum()
        {
            // If two large negative integers are provided that do not fall below int.MinValue when summed (e.g., -1,000,000 and -2,000,000), then the method should return -3,000,000. This ensures precision is maintained for larger negative magnitudes.
            var result = Calculator.Add(-1_000_000, -2_000_000);
            Assert.Equal(-3_000_000, result);
        }
    }
}

     3	/// <summary>
     4	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

using Xunit;
using Calc;

namespace Calc.Tests
{

public class CalculatorTest
{
    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractPositiveIntegersReturnsDifference()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is 10 and the second integer 'b' is 5, then the method Sub(a, b) shall return 5. This tests the core behavior of subtracting the second integer from the first for standard positive values.
        Assert.Equal(5, Calculator.Sub(10, 5));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractEqualIntegersReturnsZero()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is equal to the second integer 'b' (e.g., both are 7), then the method Sub(a, b) shall return 0. This tests the behavior when the difference between the two operands is zero.
        Assert.Equal(0, Calculator.Sub(7, 7));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractLargerFromSmallerReturnsNegative()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is smaller than the second integer 'b' (e.g., a=5, b=10), then the method Sub(a, b) shall return -5. This tests that the method correctly handles cases where the result is negative.
        Assert.Equal(-5, Calculator.Sub(5, 10));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractZeroFromPositiveReturnsOriginal()
    {
        // If the second integer 'b' is 0 and the first integer 'a' is a positive integer (e.g., a=10, b=0), then the method Sub(a, b) shall return 10. This tests the identity property of subtraction where subtracting zero leaves the first operand unchanged.
        Assert.Equal(10, Calculator.Sub(10, 0));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractPositiveFromZeroReturnsNegation()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is 0 and the second integer 'b' is a positive integer (e.g., a=0, b=5), then the method Sub(a, b) shall return -5. This tests the behavior of subtracting a positive number from zero.
        Assert.Equal(-5, Calculator.Sub(0, 5));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractPositiveFromNegativeReturnsMoreNegative()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is negative and the second integer 'b' is positive (e.g., a=-5, b=3), then the method Sub(a, b) shall return -8. This tests subtraction involving mixed signs where the magnitude increases in the negative direction.
        Assert.Equal(-8, Calculator.Sub(-5, 3));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractNegativeFromPositiveReturnsSumOfMagnitudes()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is positive and the second integer 'b' is negative (e.g., a=5, b=-3), then the method Sub(a, b) shall return 8. This tests subtraction where subtracting a negative number effectively adds its magnitude.
        Assert.Equal(8, Calculator.Sub(5, -3));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractNegativeFromNegativeReturnsMathematicalDifference()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is negative and the second integer 'b' is negative (e.g., a=-5, b=-3), then the method Sub(a, b) shall return -2. This tests subtraction where both operands are negative.
        Assert.Equal(-2, Calculator.Sub(-5, -3));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractZeroFromMinValueReturnsMinValue()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is int.MinValue and the second integer 'b' is 0, then the method Sub(a, b) shall return int.MinValue. This tests the boundary condition of the minimum integer value without overflow.
        Assert.Equal(int.MinValue, Calculator.Sub(int.MinValue, 0));
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubtractZeroFromMaxValueReturnsMaxValue()
    {
        // If the first integer 'a' is int.MaxValue and the second integer 'b' is 0, then the method Sub(a, b) shall return int.MaxValue. This tests the boundary condition of the maximum integer value without overflow.
        Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue, Calculator.Sub(int.MaxValue, 0));
    }
}
}

     4	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

using Xunit;
using Calc;

namespace Calc.Tests
{

public class CalculatorTest
{
    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnProductOfPositiveIntegers()
    {
        // If Mul is called with two positive integers (e.g., 2 and 3), then it should return their mathematical product (6). This verifies the core 'multiplies two integers' functionality for standard positive inputs.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(2, 3);
        Assert.Equal(6, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldHandleNegativeAndPositiveInput()
    {
        // If Mul is called with a negative integer and a positive integer (e.g., -2 and 3), then it should return the negative product (-6). This tests the sign handling logic described by 'multiplies two integers'.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(-2, 3);
        Assert.Equal(-6, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldHandleTwoNegativeInputs()
    {
        // If Mul is called with two negative integers (e.g., -2 and -3), then it should return the positive product (6). This verifies correct sign arithmetic for negative operands.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(-2, -3);
        Assert.Equal(6, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnZeroWhenFirstOperandIsZero()
    {
        // If Mul is called with 0 as the first operand and any integer as the second (e.g., 0 and 5), then it should return 0. This tests the identity property of zero multiplication.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(0, 5);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnZeroWhenSecondOperandIsZero()
    {
        // If Mul is called with any integer as the first operand and 0 as the second (e.g., 5 and 0), then it should return 0. This tests the commutative property of zero multiplication.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(5, 0);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnFirstOperandWhenSecondIsOne()
    {
        // If Mul is called with any integer as the first operand and 1 as the second (e.g., 5 and 1), then it should return the first operand (5). This tests the multiplicative identity property.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(5, 1);
        Assert.Equal(5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnFirstOperandWhenFirstIsOne()
    {
        // If Mul is called with 1 as the first operand and any integer as the second (e.g., 1 and 5), then it should return the second operand (5). This tests the commutative property of the multiplicative identity.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(1, 5);
        Assert.Equal(5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnNegatedFirstOperandWhenSecondIsNegativeOne()
    {
        // If Mul is called with any integer as the first operand and -1 as the second (e.g., 5 and -1), then it should return the negated first operand (-5). This tests sign inversion logic.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(5, -1);
        Assert.Equal(-5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldHandleMaxValueAndOne()
    {
        // If Mul is called with int.MaxValue and 1, then it should return int.MaxValue. This tests boundary behavior at the upper limit of the integer range without overflow.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(int.MaxValue, 1);
        Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldHandleMinValueAndOne()
    {
        // If Mul is called with int.MinValue and 1, then it should return int.MinValue. This tests boundary behavior at the lower limit of the integer range without overflow.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(int.MinValue, 1);
        Assert.Equal(int.MinValue, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldHandleMaxValueAndZero()
    {
        // If Mul is called with int.MaxValue and 0, then it should return 0. This ensures zero multiplication works correctly even at boundary values.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(int.MaxValue, 0);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldHandleMinValueAndZero()
    {
        // If Mul is called with int.MinValue and 0, then it should return 0. This ensures zero multiplication works correctly even at boundary values.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(int.MinValue, 0);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldWrapOnPositiveOverflow()
    {
        // If Mul is called with int.MaxValue and 2 (which exceeds int.MaxValue), then it should return -2. Since the documentation does not specify throwing an OverflowException, the method must follow standard unchecked integer arithmetic behavior, where overflow wraps around.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(int.MaxValue, 2);
        Assert.Equal(-2, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldWrapOnNegativeOverflowMinValueTimesNegativeOne()
    {
        // If Mul is called with int.MinValue and -1, then it should return int.MinValue. This specific case (MinValue * -1) cannot be represented in a signed 32-bit integer. Since no exception is documented, the method must return the wrapped result (which is MinValue due to two's complement symmetry limitation).
        int result = Calculator.Mul(int.MinValue, -1);
        Assert.Equal(int.MinValue, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldWrapOnNegativeOverflowMinValueTimesTwo()
    {
        // If Mul is called with int.MinValue and 2, then it should return 0. This verifies that overflow behavior is consistent with standard unchecked integer arithmetic for large negative products.
        int result = Calculator.Mul(int.MinValue, 2);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldBeCommutativeForPositiveIntegers()
    {
        // If Mul(a, b) is called with positive integers, then the result should be equal to Mul(b, a). This verifies the mathematical commutative property of multiplication.
        int a = 123;
        int b = 456;
        int result1 = Calculator.Mul(a, b);
        int result2 = Calculator.Mul(b, a);
        Assert.Equal(result1, result2);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldBeCommutativeForNegativeIntegers()
    {
        // If Mul(a, b) is called with negative integers, then the result should be equal to Mul(b, a). This verifies the commutative property holds for negative inputs.
        int a = -123;
        int b = -456;
        int result1 = Calculator.Mul(a, b);
        int result2 = Calculator.Mul(b, a);
        Assert.Equal(result1, result2);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldBeCommutativeForMixedSignIntegers()
    {
        // If Mul(a, b) is called with one positive and one negative integer, then the result should be equal to Mul(b, a). This verifies the commutative property holds for mixed-sign inputs.
        int a = 123;
        int b = -456;
        int result1 = Calculator.Mul(a, b);
        int result2 = Calculator.Mul(b, a);
        Assert.Equal(result1, result2);
    }
}

     1	namespace Calc;
     2	
     3	/// <summary>
     4	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

     1	namespace Calc;
     2	/// <summary>
     3	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     4	/// </summary>
     5	public class Calculator
     6	{
     7	    /// <summary>
     8	    /// Adds two integers.
     9	    /// </summary>
    10	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    11	    {
    12	        return a + b;
    13	    }
    14	
    15	    /// <summary>
    16	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    17	    /// </summary>
    18	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    19	    {
    20	        return a - b;
    21	    }
    22	
    23	    /// <summary>
    24	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    25	    /// </summary>
    26	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    27	    {
    28	        return a * b;
    29	    }
    30	}     1
